Item No. 4: Minimum Remuneration paid to Ms. Irmgard Velagapudi (DIN:00091370) 
as Managing Director of the Company 
Ms. lrmgarq Velagapudl, Managing Director, has been paid Minimum Remuneration by way 
of Salary and Perquisites for the period 01/04/2024 to 31/03/2025 being the fifth financial 
year of inadequate profits durfng her tenure covering 29/03/.2020 to 28/0312025. On 
finalization of Accounts, for the Financial Year 2024 - 2025, it was ascertained that the 
Company has sustained loss for the said Financial Year. As per Schedule V of the 
Companies Act, 2013, Minimum Remuneration can be paid for a period not exceeding three 
years, during the years of inadequate profits falling underlhe tenure of the director. 
However, in terms of Section 197(10) of the Companies Act, :1013, the Minimum 
Remuneration paid to Managerial Personnel beyond the saJd period of three years may be 
ratified by the Shareholders by passing a ·Special Resolution . 
The Nomination and Remuneration Committee of the Board of Directors of the Company 
has recommended the ratification or the payment or the Minimum Remuneration paid for 
the Financial Year 2024 - 2025 td the Managing Dir,ector and the said recommendation of 
Nomination and Remuneration Committee is approved by the Board at Its Meeting held on 
28/05/2025. Now the matter is place befon~ the Shareholders for ratification of payment of 
Minimu1T1 Remuneration to the Managing Director for the Financial Year 2024 - 2025. 
Ms. Irmgard Velagapudl ls interested in the resolution set out in Item No. 4 or the Notice of 
Annual General Meeting. None of the other Directors and Key Managerial Personnel of the 
Company except Ms. Kiran Velagapudi and Mr. Vlnod R. Sethi, Executive. Chairman being 
relatives, is deemed to be concerned in the said Item No. 4.

MANAGEMENT DISCUSSION AND ANALYSIS REPORT 
{PursuaJJt to Regulation 34 (3) read with Schedule V of SEBI (LODR) Regulations, 2015] 
1. INDUSTRY STRUCTURE AND DEVELOPMENTS: 
Sugar Industry Is the second largest agro-based Industry in India·. It conlributes significantly to the 
socio economic development of the nation. tt Impacts lhe livelihood of large number of farmers and 
their dependents. This industry ts·very incomparably sett-reliant. Over the years It has proven to be 
one of the largest contributors to a naflon's economy. The sustainability of Sugar Industry ln India Is 
largely dependent on supply and·demand which often used to mismatch on account of its cyclicality. 
Sugarcane and sugar production are seasonal with substantial production cycle wltnesstid In the 
months of October to April, 
The cyclic and seasonally driven factors in the industry are highly dependent ori a stable Government 
policy and favourable sugar market dynamics .which can aid towards Improving sugar profitability. 
The Government has been in concerting efforts by encouraging diversion of excess sugarcane and 
sugar to ethanol for blending purposes which not only reduce .Import dependency on crude-oil, but 
promote ethanol as a fuel that is Indigenous and environment-friendly and will also enhance the 
income of sugarcane farmers. Government of India's thrust for ethanol prqduction will enable lhe 
sugar millers to divert surplus sugar towards ethanol manul-acturing, thus reducing the cyclicality in 
s(!gar business. 
The Industry has also invented manufacturing of 'Green Manure' for application in agricultural fields 
In order to improve soil fertility an.d sell health management by partially removing the application of 
inorganic fertilisers. This Green Manure called blo compost is obtained from a waste product while 
manufacturing sugar namely, 'press mud'. Several micro nutrients and treated blo waste obtained 
from distillery efflL1ent are applied on the said press mud to convert the same as Green Manure. 
This ls.gaining more Importance In India as the said Gree~ Manure has proven the Improvement of 
soil fertnity and development of eco-friendly atmosphere. 
The Production of sugars In the Southern region of India has toppletl down drastically when 
compared io other major sugar produeing regions in India and successive years of drought and 
lower level of recovery of sugar, have incapacitated the staying power of several private players 
lea·ding to close down of sugar m111s over the period in various southern regions of1he nation. 
2. OPPORTUNITIESANDTHREATS: 
(I) Opportunities: 
The long lerrn outlook for Sugar Industry remains positive and promising on account. of: 
► Growing robust and sustainable demand of ethanol wfll tioosl the revenue and profitability 
of Sugar MIiis; 
► Envlrof)mental friendly method of powe·r generation adopted by Cogeneratlon Units 
equipped with high-pressure boilers·and turbines intelligently uses lhe fuel to gel optimum 
energy output; and 
► Emphasize on blo-composting process by converting distillery, discharge into ·organic 
matter combining with press mud for application in agricultu[e lo enhance the fertility of the 
soil. 
(II) Threats: 
The Sugar industry is at present confronted by the following threats; 
> Risk of surge in sugarcane prices and harvesting expenses.

(ii) Polley Risk:

State Governments as monitoring agenc1es. Therefore, the performance of the Sugar Mills will

In close liaison with SISMA.

the p;ofitabifity. Sugar being a commodity traded across the world, Its price is Influenced by 
the various factors Including the normal supply and demand. 
Mitigation Measure: 
The Company focuses on value-added downstream (!)roducts and integrates sugar with 
electricity and alcohol thereby insulating itself against price risk.

1. COMPANY'S PHILOSOPHY ON CODE OF GOVERNANCE:

corporate practices. constariUy striving to keep ln place evolving system lo cope up with the core 
standards of transparency, accountability and disclosure in true letter and spirit. The Company is 
deeplycommltted to effect and uphold the core values of corporate governance by adhering to iaw

Auditor's Responsibilities for the Audit of the Standalone Financial Statements 
Our objectives are to obtain reasonable assurance about whether the financial statements as a whole 
are free from material misstatement. whether due to fraud or error, and to lssl.(e an auditor's report that 
Includes our opinion. Reasonable assurance is a high level of assurance, b\,11 rs not a guarantee lhat an 
audit conducted in accordance with SAs will always detect a material misstatement when it exists. 
Mfsstatements can arise from fraud or error and are considered material if, individually or in the 
aggregate, they could reasonably be expected to influence the ecoRo,rilc decisiens of users taken on 
\he basis of these standalone financial statements. 
As part or an audit in accordance with SAs, we exercise professional judgment and maintain 
proressignal skepticism throughout the audit. We also: 
• Identify and assess the risks of material misstatement of the financial statements. whether due to 
fraud or error, design and perfom1 audit proeedures responsive to those risks, and obtain audit 
·evidence that is sufficient and appropriate to provid'e a basis for our opinion. The risk of not 
detecting a material mls~tatement resulting from fn\ud rs higher than for one resulting from .error. 
as fraud may Involve collusion, forgery, intentional omissions, misrepresentations, or the override 
or internal control.

ANNEXURE B TO THE INDEPENDENT AUDITOR'S REPORT ON THE STANDALONE FINANCIAL 
STATEMENTS OF K.C.P. SUGAR AND INDUSTRIES CORPORATION LIMITED FOR THE YEAR 
ENDED MARCH 31, 2025 
Report on the Internal Financial Controls· Over Financial Reporting under Clause (I) of 
Subsection 3 of Section 143 of th,e Companies Act, 201 3 ("the Act") 
{Referred to in paragraph 2(f) under 'Report on Other Legal and Regulatory Requirements· section of 
our report of even date) 
We have.audited the internal financial controls over financial reporting of K.C.P. Sugar and Indus.tries 
Corporation Limited ("the Company.") as of March 31, 2025 in conjunction wllh our audit of the 
standalone financial statements of the Company for the year ended on that date. 
Management's and Board of Directors Responsibilities for Internal Financial Co(ltrols 
The Company's Management and lhe Board of Directors are responsible for establishing and 
maintaining internal financial controls based on the internal control over finant::ial reporting criteria 
established by the Company considering the essential components of internal control stated in the 
Guidance Note on Audit of Internal Financial Controls Over Financial Reporting issued by the Institute 
of Chartered Accountants of India. T~ese resp9nsibllitles Include lhe design, .Jmplemenlatlon and 
maintenance of adequate internal financial controls that• were operating effectively for ensuring the 
orderly and efficient conduct of its business; the safeguarding of its assets, the prevention and detection 
of frauds and errors, the accuracy and coinpleteness of the accounting records, and the timely 
preparation of reliable financial information, as required under the Companies Act. 2013. 
Auditor's Responsibility 
Our responsibility is lo express an oplnibn on the internal financial controls over financial repofllng of 
the Company based oh our audit. We conducted oar audit In accordance wlth the Guidance Note on 
' Audit of Internal Financial Controls Over Financial Reporting (the "Guidance Note") issued by the 
lnstitule of Chartered Accountants of India and the ·standards on Auditing prescribed under 
Sectlon143(10) of the Companies Act. 2013, to the extent applicable to ao audit of internal financial 
controls. 
Those ~nda rds and the Guidance Note require that we C9mply with ethical requirements and plan 
and perform the audit lo obtain reasonable assurance about whether adequate lnternal financlai 
controls over financial reporting was established and maintained and if such controls operated 
effectively In all material respects. 
Our audit involves performing procedures to obtain audit evidence about the adequacy o( the internal 
financial controls system over financial reporting and their operating effectiveness. Our audit of internal 
financial controls o.ver financial reporting included obtaining an understanding of lntema'I financial 
controls over financial• reporting, assessing the risk that a material weakness exists, and testing and 
evaluating the design and operatlng effectiveness of internal control based on lhe assessed risk. The 
prC>cedures selected depend on the auditor's judgment, including lhe assessment of the risks of material 
misstatement of the finanoiai statements. whether due to fraud or error. 
Wf3 believe that the audit evidence we have obtained is sufficient a[ld appropriate to provide a basis for 
our audit opinion on the Company's Internal financial controls system.over financial reporting. 
Meaning of Internal Financial Controls Over Financial Reporting 
A· company's Internal financial control over financial reporting is a process designed lo provide 
reasonable assurance regarding the reliability of finariclal reporting and the preparation of standalone 
financial statements for external purposes in accordance with generally aooepted accounting principles. 
A company's Internal financia.l control over financial reporting Includes those policies and procedures 
that 
(1) Per.lain to the maintenance of records that. In reasonable detail. accurately and fairly reflect 1he 
transactlons and dispositions of the assets of the company:

Limitations of Internal Financial Controls over Flnanclal Reporting 
Because of the ;nherent limitations of Internal flnar\cfal controls over financial reporting, Including the 
possibility of collusion or improper management override of controls, materlal misstatements due lo 
error or fraud may occur and not be detected. Also, proJeclions of any evaluallon of the internal flnancfal 
controls over financial reporting lo future periods are subject to the risk that t~e internal flnanclal control 
over financial reporting may become Inadequate because.of changes In condllions, or that the degree 
of compliance with the policies or procedures may deteriorate.

the,essential components of interna1·control stated in the Guidance Note on· Audll of Internal Financial

1. Corporate Information 
K.C.P Sugar and Industries Corporation Ltd Is a listed entity, one an,ong the leading 
sugar manufacturing companies In India .The Company has a Sugar factory at 
Vuyyuru, Krishna District, Andhra Pradesh with a sugarcane crushing capacity of 
7500 tons per day. Its allied business consists of m~nufacturing and maiketlng of 
Recl1fied Spirit, Ethanol, Incidental Co.generation of Power. Organic Manure. 
Mycorr~iza Varn, CaichJ1n Lactate and Co2. Processing of Urad Dal, Surgical Sprit, 
Engineering unit at Thuvakudy, Tamil Nadu. It has Its registered office al 239/183, 
Ramakrishna Buildings, Anna Sala!, Chennal, Tamil Nadu 600006, India. 
The financial s·tatements were approved by U1e Board of Directors and authorised to 
issue on 28/05/2025 
2. Significant Accounting Policies 
(a) Statement of compliance: 
The financial statements of the Company have been prepared In accordance with 
Indian Accounting Standards ("Ind AS") notified under the Companies (Indian 
Accounting Standards) Rules, 2015, notified under Sec 133 or The Companies 
Act, 2013. The Accounting policies have been consistently applied except where 
a newly Issued accounting standard fs initially adopted or a revision to an exJsling 
standard requires a change in the accounting policies hitherto in use. 
(b) Basis of preparation and presentat.ion: 
These financial statements have been prepared on a historical cost basis, 
except for certain financial instruments anq net defined benefit liability that are 
measured at fair value at the end of each reporting period, as explained in the 
accounting policies below. 
(c) Critical accounting estimates and jud.gments 
The preparallon of financial statements In conformity with lndAS requires 
management to make judgements, estin1ates and assumptions that affect the 
appllcatien o.f accounting polieles and thereported amounts of assets and 
liabilities, disclosures of contingent liabilities at the date or the financial 
statements and the reported amounts of revenue and expenses for the years 
presented. Actual results may d1ffer from these estimates. 
Estin,ates and underlying assumptions are reviewed on an ongoing basis. 
Revisions to accounting estimates are recognised fn lhe period In which the 
estimates are revised and in any future periods affected. 
In particular, information about significant areas of estimation, uncertainty and 
criUcal Judgments In applying accounllng policies that have the most significant 
effect on the amounts recognized In the financial statements pertain to: 
Useful lives of property, plant and equipment and intangible assets: The 
Gompany has estimated useful life of each class of assets based on the nattJre of 
assets, the estimated usage of the asset, the operating condition of the asset, 
past history or replacement, anticipated technological changes, etc. The 
Company reviews the carrying amount of property, plant and equipment and 
Intangible assets al the Balance Sheet date. This reassessment n1ay result in 
change In depreciation expense in future periods.

Impairment testing: Property. plant and equipment and Intangible assets are 
tested for impairment when events occur or changes In circumstances Indicate 
that the recoverable amount of the cash generating unit Is less than Its carrying 
value. The recoverable amount of cash generating units Is higher of value-in-use 
and fair value less cost to sell. The calculation Involves use of slgnlllcant 
estimates and assumptions which includes turnover and earnings multiples, 
growth rates and net margins used lo calculate projected future cash nows, risk­
adjusted discount rate, future economic and market condillons. 
Income Taxes: Deferred tax assets are recognized to the extent that It Is 
regarded as probable tha1 deductible temporary differences can be realized. The 
Company estimates deferred tax assets and liabilities based on current tax laws 
and rates and in certain cases, business plans, Including management's 
expectations regarding the manner and timing of recove·ry of the related assets . 
Changes in these estimates may affect lhe amount of deferred lax liabilities or lhe 
valuation of deferred tax assels and there the lax charge in the statement of profit 
or loss. 
Provision for tax liabilities require Judgments on lhe interpretation of tax 
legislation. developments in case law and the potential outcomes of .tax audits 
and appeals which may be subject to significant uncertainty. Therefore the actual 
results may vary from expectations resulting In adjustments to provisions. the 
valuation of deferred lax assets, cash tax settlements and therefore the tax 
charge In the statement of profit or toss. 
Fair value measurement financial instruments: The fair value of financial [nstruments 
that are not traded in an active n,arket is determined by using valuation techniques. TI1is 
Involves significant judgments to select a variety of methods and make assumptions that 
are mainly based on market conditions existing at the Balance Sheet date. Fair value of 
financial lnstn,1ments that are traded in active market .is determined from market prices as 
reduced by esUr11ated cost of trading. 
• Litigation: From time to Lime, the Con,pany is subject to legal proceedlngs, the ultin1ale 
outcome of each being alw;3ys subject to many uncertainties Inherent In litigation. A 
provision for litigation is made when il is considered probable that a payment will be made 
and the amount of the loss can be reasonably estir11ated. Si9nilicanl judgment is made 
when evaluating, among other factors, the probability of unfavorable outcome and the 
ability to make a reasonable esllmate of the amount of potential loss. Lil:lgation provisions 
are reviewed at each accounting period and revisions made-for the changes in facts and 
circumstances. 
• Defined benefit plans: The cost of the defined benefit plans and the present value of lhe 
defined benefit obligation are based on actuarial valuation using the projected unit credit 
method. An actuarial valuation involves making various assumptions that may differ from 
actual developments 1n the future. These include the determination of the discount rate. 
future salary Increases and mortality rates. Due to the complexities involved In lhe 
valuation and Its long term nature, a de~ned benefit obligation is highly sensitive to 
changes in these assumptions.All assumptions are reviewed at each Balance Sheet date.

(h) Intangible Assets: 
(I) 
Intangible assets Include cost of acquired software and designs, and cost incurred for 
development of the Company's website and certain contract acquisftfon costs. Intangible 
assets are initially n1easured at acquisition cost including any directly attributable costs of

I) the technical feasibility and Company's intention and ablllty of completing the 
project; 
ii) the probability that the project will generate future economic benefits; 
iii) lhe availability of adequate technical flnan-clal and other resources to complete 
the project; and 
Iv) the ability to n1easure the development expenditure reliably. 
Expenditure on projects which are nol yet ready for intended use are carried as Intangible 
assets under development. 
Intangible assets with finite lives are amortized over their es.timated useful economic life and 
assessed tor impairment wh·enever there is an lndlcation that the Intangible asset may be

asset, measured as lhe dr~erence between the net disposal proceeds and the carrying

Impairment of assets: 
Assets that have an indefinite useful life are not subject to amortisation and are tested 
annually for Impairment. Assets that are subject to amortisation are reviewed for impairment 
whenever events or crianges In circumstances Indicate that the carrying amount may not be 
recoverable. An impairment loss is recognised for the amount by which the asset's carrying 
amount exceeds ils recoverable amount. 
Recoverable amount is the hi~her of 'fair value less costs of disposal and value In use.In 
assessing.value in use, the estimated future cash flows are discounted to their present value 
using a pre-tax discount rate LhaL renects current market assessments of the lime value of 
money and the risks specific to the asset for which the estimates of future cash flows have not 
been adjusted. 
If the recoverable amount of an asset (or cash-generating unlt).is estimated to be less than its 
carrying amount, the carrying amount of the asset (or cash-generatfng unit) is reduced to its 
recoverable amount. An impairmenl loss is recognized immediately in lhe Statement of Profit 
and Loss. 
When an impairment loss subsequently reverses, the carrying amount of the assel.(or a 
cash-generaling unit) is inC(eased to the revised estimate of its recoverable an,ounl, but so 
that the increased carrying amount does not exceed the carrying amount that would have

(k) Assets taken on lease: 
Leases are classified as finance tease whenever the terms of the lease transfer substantially 
all the ri.sk anq rewards of ownership to the lessee. All the other leases are classified as 
operating leases. 
Operating lease pay111ents are recognized as expenditure in the Statement of Profit and Loss 
on a straight-line basis, unless another basis is n1ore representative of the time pattern of 
benefits received from the use of the assets taken on lease or the payments of lease rentals 
are in trne with the expected general infh,1tion compensating the lessor kir expected 
inflationary cost. Contingent rentals arising under operating leases are recognized as an 
expense tn the period In which they are incurred. 
Assets held under finance lease are capitalised at lhe Inception of the lease, with 
corresponding liability being recognised for the fair value of the leased assets or, if lower. the 
present value of the minimum lease payments. Lease payments are apportioned between 
the reduction of the lease liability and finance charges in the statement of Profit or Loss so as 
to achieve a constant rate of Interest on the remaining balance of the liability. A~sets held 
under finance leases are depreciated over the shorter of the estimated Useful life of the asset 
and theleaselerm.

(n) Income Taxes: 
Income tax expense comprises current tax expehse and the net change In the deferred tax 
asset or llabllity during the year. Current and deferred tax are recognised in profit or loss, 
except when they relate to items that are recognised in other comprehensive income o.r 
directly in equity, in which case, the current and deferred tax are also recognised in other 
comprehensive income or directly In equity, respectively, 
(I) Current Tax: 
Current Tax expenses are accounted in tpe same period to which the revenue and expenses 
relate. Provision for current income tax is made for the tax liability payable on taxable income 
after considering tax allowances, deductions and exemptions determined In accordance 'with 
the applicable tax rates and the prevailing tax laws. 
Current tax assets and current tax liabilities are offset when there is a legally enforceable 
right to set off the recognised amou~ts and there is an intention to settle the asset and the 
liabflity on a net basis. 
(ii) Deferred Tax : 
Deferred Income tax is recognised using the balance sheet approach. Deferred income tax 
assets and liabilities are recognised for deductible and taxable temporary differences arising 
between the tax base of assets and liabilities and their carrying amount in financial 
statements, except when lhe deferred income tax arises from the inltlal recognition of 
goodwill, an asset or llabllity 1n a transaction that is not a business combination and affects 
neither accounting nor taxable profits or loss at the time of the transaction. 
Deferred 1ncon1e tax assets are recognised to the extent that it is probable that taxable profit 
wlll be available against whit:11 the deductible temporary differences and the carry forward or 
unused tax credits and unused tax losses can be utilised. 
Deferred tax liabilities are generally recognized for all taxable temporary differences except 
In respect of taxable temporary differences associated with Investments In subsidiaries. 
associates and interests in joint ventures where the liming of the reversal of the temporary 
difference can be controlled and It is probable that the temporary difference will not reverse In 
the foreseeable future. 
The carrying an,ount of deferred tax assets is reviewed at each Balance Sheet date and 
reduced to the extent that It is no longer probable that sufficient tc1xable profits will be 
available lo allow all or part of the deferred incon1e tax asset to be utilised.

(o) Accounting for Provisions, Contingent Liabilities and Contingent Assets: 
Provisions are recognized, when there is a present legal or construcfive obligation as a result 
of a past event, tt Is probable that an outflow of resources will be required to settle the 
obligation, and when a reliable estimate of the amount of the obligation can be made. If lhe 
effect of the time value of money Is material, the prov.islon is discounted using a pre-tax rate 
that reflects current market assessments of lhe lime value of money and the risks specific lo 
the obligation and the unwinding of the discount is recognised as Interest expense. 
Contingent liabilities are recognized only when there Is a possible ebllgation arising from 
past events, due lo occurrence or non.occurrence or one or more uncertain future events, not 
wholly within the control of the Company, or where any present obligation cannot be 
measured In terms of future outflow of resources, or where a reliable estimate of lhe 
obligation cannotbe made. Obligations are assessed on an ongoing bas.is and only those 
having a largely probable outflow of resources are provided for. Contingent liabilities are not 
recognrzed In these financial statements, butare disclosed in Note No.45. 
Contingent assets are not recognized in the financial statements.

(s ) Earnings Per Share: 
Basic earnings per share is computed by dividing the profit / (loss) after tax (including the 
post-tax effect of extraordinary items, If any) by the weighted average number of equity 
shares outstanding during the year Including potential equity shares on compulsory 
convertible debentures. Dllute.d earnings per share is computed by divldln_g the profit/ (loss) 
after tax (Including the post-tax effect of extraordinary Items, ff any) as adjusted for dividend. 
Interest and other charges lo expense or Income (net of any attributable truces) relaling to lhe 
dilutive potential equity shares, by the weighted average number of equity shares considered 
for deriving basic earnings per share and the weighted average number of equity shares 
which could have been Issued on the conversion of all dilutive potential equity shares. 
(t) Segment Reporting: 
The Company ldenttfies operating segments based on the internal reporting provided to the

The Managing Director, who is r~ponsible for allocating resources and assessing

The accounting policies adopted for segment reporting are in line with the aGcounting 
policies of 1he Company. Segmenl revenue. segment expenses, segment assets and 
segment liabilities have been identified to segments on the basis of their relationship to the 
operating acttvities of the segment. 
Inter-segment revenue is accounted on the basis of transactions which are primarily 
determined based on market/ fair value factors. Revenue, expenses, assets and liabilities 
which relate to the Company as a whole and are not allocable to segments on reasonable 
basis have been included under "unallocated revenue/ expenses/ assets/liabilities". 
All operating seg1nents, operating results are reviewed regularly by the Company'.s Board of 
Directors lo ma~e decisions about resources lo be allocated to the segments and assess 
their perforn1ance.

After initial measurement, such financial assets are subsequently measured at amortised 
cost using the effective interest rate (EIR) method.An1ortised cost is calculated by taking into 
account any discount or premium and fees or costs that are an Integral part of the EIR. The 
EI R amortisaUon is included in finance income in the Staten1ent of Pr.ofit and Loss. The 
losses arising from Impairment are recognised In the statement of Profil and loss. This 
category generally applies lo loans and advances, deposits, trade and other receivables. 
Debt Instruments Included within the fair value through profit and loss (FVTPL) category are 
measured at fair value with all changes recognized in !he Statement of Prefit and Loss. 
Eauity investments 
All equity inveshnents in scope of Ind-AS 109 are 1neasured at fair value. Equity instruments 
are classified as FVTPL. Investment in subsidiaries, joint ventures and associates are 
carried at costless impairment, 1fany. 
Derecognition 
A financial assel (or, Where applicable, a part of a financial asset or part of a group of similar 
financial assets) is primarily derecognlsed (i.e. removed from the Company's balance sheet) 
when: 
• The rights lo receive cash flows from the asset have e~plred, or 
• The Company has transferred its rights to receive cash flows from the asset or 
has assun1ed an obligation to pay the received cash flows in full without material 
delay lo a third party under a 'pass-through' arrangement; and either: 
(a). lhe Company has transferred substantially all the risks and rewards oflhe asset, or 
())) lhe Company has nellher transferred nor retained substantially all the risks and 
rewards of the asset, but has transferred control of the asset. 
When lhe Company tias lra,nsferred Its rights to receive cash rlo,vs fron, an asset or tias 
entered Into a pass-through arrangement, it evaluates If and to what extent\! has retained the 
risks and rewards of ownetship. When It has neither transferred nor retained substantially all 
of the risks and rewards of the asset, nor transferred control of the asset, the Company 
continues to recognise the transferred asset to the extent of the Company's continuing 
involvement. In that case, the Company also recognises an associated liability. The 
transferred asset and the associated liability are measured on a basis that reflects the rights 
and obligations that the Company has retained. 
Con.llnuing involvement lhat lakes the form of a guarar:itee over the (ransferre\:l asset is 
n1easured at the lower of the original carrying amount of the asset and the 1naxin1um amount 
of consideration that the Company could be required to repay.

Financial Liabilities 
Classification 
The Company classifie.s all financial liabilities as subsequently measured at amortised cost. 
except for financial liabilities al fair value through profit or toss. Such liabilities, including 
derivatives that are liabilities. shall be subsequently measured atfairvatue 
Initial recognition and meai;;urement. 
Financial liabilities are classified. al initial recegnition, as financial liabilities at fair value 
through profit or loss, loans and b0rrowlngs, payables, o.r as derivatives designated as 
hedging Instruments In an effective hedge, as appropriate. 
All financial liabilities are recognised Initially at fair value and, In the case of loans and 
borrowings and payables, net of directly attributable transaction costs. 
The Company.'s financial llabilllies include trade and other payables. loans and borrowings 
including bank overdrafts, ·nnanclal guarantee contracts and derivative financial Instruments. 
(,oans and borcowiogs 
After initial recognition, lnte,rest-bearing loans and borrowfngs are slJbsequenlly measured 
at amortised cost using the El R n1ethod. Gains and losses are recognised in the Statement 
of Profit and Loss \vhen the liabilities are derecognised. 
Amortised cost is calcuJated by taking into account any discount or pren1iun1 on acqu.isition 
and fees or costs that are an Integral part of the EIR. The EIR amortisation Is included as 
finance costs in the Statement of Profit and Loss. 
This category generally applies to interest-bearing loans and borrowings. 
Derecognition 
A financial liablllty is derecognlsed when the obligation under the liability Is discharged or 
cancelled or expires. When an existing financial llabillty ls replaced by another from the same 
lender on substanlially different terms. or the terms of an existing liability are substantially 
modified, such an exchange or modification Is treated as the derecognition of the original 
liability and the recognition oi a new liability. The difference In the respective canylng 
amounts is recognised in the Statement of Profit and Loss. 
Offsetting of financial 'instruments 
Financial assets and financial llabilltles are offset and the nel amount Is reported In the 
balance sheet if there is a currently enforceable legal right to offset the recognised amounts 
and there ls an inlenfion to settle on a net basis, to realise the assets and settle the llabllllles 
sln,ultaneously.

entity afier deducting all of H:s liabilities. Equity instruments issued by the Company are 
recognized at the proceeds received, net of direct issue costs.

b. Fair Value Hierarchy 
• Level 1 - Quoted prices (unadjusted) In acllve markets for identical.assets or liabilities. 
• Level 2 • Inputs other than quoted prices Included within Level 1 that are observable ror the 
,asset or liability, ellher directly (i.e. as prices) or indirectly O,e. derived from prices). 
• Level 3 - Inputs for the assets or II.abilities that arEi not based on observable n1arkel data 
(unobserv.able inputs).

49. Financial risk management 
The .Company's activities expose to,Umited financial ris.ks: market risk, credrl risk and liquidity risk. 
The Company's primary focus is to foresee lhe unprediclabllify of financial markets and seek lo 
mlnln1lze potential adverse effects on Its financial perfom,ance. 
Market risk 
Markel ris.k• is the risk or l,oss of future earnings or (air values or future cash flows lhal may result 
from a change in lhe price of a financial instrumenl 
The co,mpany Is expose'il to market ,risk primarily related to foreign exchange rate risk '(currency 
risk). Interest rate risk and the market value of its Investments. 
Sec-urities Prices Risk: 
The company's exposure to equity securities price risk arises from Investments held and classified 
in the Balance Sheet as Fair Value through P&L. The company has investment In the form of 
Mutual funds and Equity shares. The company monitors the movement In the Value ofihe 
Investments by observing the NAV. 
Credit Risk 
Credlt risk refe~ to lt,e fisk of default on Its obligation by the counterparty resulting in a financial 
loss. It principally arises from the Company's Trade Receivables, Advances and deposit(s) made. 
Trade recefvables 
Theco1T1pany has outstanding trade receivables amounting to Rs.1177.61 Lakhs and Rs.1329.20 Lakhs 
as of Maret, 31, 2025 and March 31, 2024 respectively. Trade receivables are typically unsec.ured are 
derived from revenue earned from customers. Company's eirposure to credit risk Is influenced mainly by 
lhe individual oharacterisUcsofeach customer. The company is not exposed lo aoncenlraUon of credit risk 
to any one single customer. Default on aooount of Trade Receivables happens when the oourrterparty fails 
to make contracllJal payment when they fall due. 
Further for amounts overdue are consta11Uy monitored oy the mana_gemenl and provision lowards 
e.11pected credit loss are rnade in the books. Management eslimate.d pf expeotecl credit loss forlhe Trade 
Receivables are provided belowwilh the classlficatlon on debtors.

Capital managements 
The Company's objectives when managing capital are lo safeguard the Company's ability to continue as 
a going concern In order lo provide returns for shareholders a(ld benefits for other stakeholders and to 
malntai_n an optimal capital structure. 
In order to maintain or adjust the cap'rtal stnact~rre, the Company may adjust the amount of dividends paid 
.to shareholders. return capital to shareholders, issue new shares or sell assets or by adequate funding by 
the shareholders to aosorb the losses of the Company . 
1 he Company's capllal comprises equity share capital, retained earnings and other equity attributable 10 
equity holders. The primary objective of Company's capital management is ,lo maximize shareholders 
value. The Company manages its aapital and makes adjustment to it in light of the changes in economic

INDEPENDENT AUDITOR'S REPORT 
To the memb.ers of THE EIMCO K.C.P. LIMITED 
Report on the Audit of the Standalone Financial Statements 
Opinion 
We have audited the standalone financial statements of THE EIMCO K.C.P. LIMITED ("the Company"), 
which comprise the slandafone balance sheet as al March 31, 2025, the standalone statement or profit 
and loss (Including other comprehel)sive income ), the standalone statement of changes in equity and 
standalone statement of cash flows for the year then ended, and notes to the standalone financial 
statements, including a sumn1ary of significant accounting policies and other explanatory information 
(hereinafter referred to as the ·standalone financial statements"). 
In our opinion and to the best of our in'formation and according to the explanations given to us, the 
aforesaid standalone financial statements give the information requl~ by the Companies Act, 2013 
('the Act") in the manner so required and give a true and fair view in conformity with the Indian 
Accounting Standards prescribed under sec 133 ot the Act read with the Companies (lndlan Accounting 
Standards)Rules.2015,as amended,(.lfld AS.") and other aceounting principles generally accepted In 
India, of the state of affairs of1he Company as at March 31, 2025, its profit and other comprehensive 
income, ils cash flows and the changes in equity for the year ended on that date. 
Basis for Opinion 
We conducted our audit of the standalone 'financial stateme_nts In accordance wit~ the S1anpai'ds on 
Auditing specified under section 143(10) of the Companies Act 2013 (SAs). Our responsibilities under 
those Standards are further described In the Auditor's Responsibilities for U1a Audit of the Standalone 
Financial Statements section of our report. We are independent of the Company In accordance with the 
Code of Ethics- Issued by the Institute of Chartered Accountants of India (!CAI) together with the 
Independence re9uirements that are relevant to our audit of the standalone financial statements under 
the provlsion.s of the Companies Act 2013 and the Rules thereunder, arid we have fulfilled our other 
ethical responsibilities in accordance with these requlrernen'ls and the !CAI 's Code of Ethics. We believe 
that the audit evidence we have obtained by us is sllfficient and appropriate to provide a basis for our 
audit opinion on the standalone financial statements. 
Key AudJt Matters 
Key audit matters are those matters that, In our professional judgmenl were of most significance in our 
audH of the standalone financial statements of the current period. Tliese matters were addressed in the 
context of our audll of the standalone financial statements as a whole, and In forming our opinion 
thereon, and we do not provide a separate opinion on these matters. We nave determined that there 
are no key audit matters to be communicated in our audit report. 
Information Other than the Standalone Financial Statements and Auditor's Report Thereon 
The Company's Management and Board of Directors are responsible for the othet information. The 
other Information comprises the Information Included In the Management Discussion and Analysis, 
Board's Report including Annexures to Board's Report, Corporate Governance and Shareholder's 
Information. but does not include the star,dalone financial ~tatements and our auditor's report thereon. 
Our opinion on the standalone financial statements does not cover the other information and we do not 
e)!:press any form of assuranc;e conclusion thereon. 
In c;:onneelion with our audit of the standalone financial statements, our responsibility is to read the other 
inrormation ahd, in doin.g so, consider whether the other lnformalion is materially inconsistent with the 
standalone financial state,n.enls or our knowledge obtained clurlng the course of our audit or olhe.rwise 
appears to be 1naterialiy n1isstated.

including other co·mprehensive income r loss, cnanges in,,equlty and cash nows of the Company in 
accordance with 1:iccounLing princfples generally accepted In India, Including tile Indian Accounting 
Standards (Ind AS}specified under section 133 of !he Act. This responsibility also includes maintenance 
or adequate accounting records in accordance with lhe provisions of ihe Act ·for safeguarding of the

Auditor's Responsibilities for the Audit of the Standalone Financial Statements 
Our objectives are to obtain reasonable assurance about whether the standalone financial statements 
as a whole are free from-material misstatement, whether due to fraud or error, and to issue an auditor's 
report that Includes our opinion. Reasonable assurance Is a high level of assurance bul is not a 
guarantee Iha! a11 audit conducted In accordance with SAs will always detect a material misstatement 
wheh it exists. Misstatements can arise from fraud or error and are,consldered material If, Individually 
or In the aggregate, they could reasonably be expected to influence .the economic decisions of users 
taken on the basis of these standalone financial statements. 
As part of an audit in accordance with SAs. we exercise professional judg_ment and maintain 
professlonal skepticism throughout the audit. We also: 
• Identify and assess the risks of material misstatement of the standalone financial statements, 
whether due to fraud or error, design and perform audit procedures responsive to those risks, 
and obtain audit evidence that is sufficient and appropriil-\e to provide a basis for our opinion.
